Pair creation, backreaction and resummation in strong fields
Patrick Copinger, James P. Edwards, Anton Ilderton, Karthik Rajeev

Abstract
We revisit particle creation in strong fields, and backreaction on those fields, from an amplitudes perspective. We describe the strong field by an initial coherent state of photons which we explicitly evolve in time, thus going beyond the background field approximation, and then consider observables which quantify the effects of backreaction. We present expressions for the waveform, vacuum persistence probability and number of produced photons at next-to-leading-order, all of which are impacted by backreaction, along with the number and statistics of produced pairs. We find that converting between in-out (amplitude) and in-in (expectation value) expressions requires explicit resummation of an infinite number of disconnected loop diagrams.



I
Introduction

The intense electromagnetic fields of modern lasers provide opportunities for studying QED in the largely untested strong-field regime
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
]
. The majority of theoretical approaches to ‘strong field QED’ (SFQED) use the Furry expansion
[
6
]
, in which the strong field is represented as a fixed, classical background, about which field perturbations are assumed small. For reviews see
[
7
,
8
]
. This is a good approximation in a broad range of scenarios. However, backreaction on the field, in the form of e.g. beam-depletion, is expected to become important in collisions of high-charge bunches with very intense laser fields
[
9
]
, and in high-multiplicity cascades where significant energy is taken from the driving fields
[
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
]
. It may also be necessary to account for backreaction in the high-intensity regime where the loop expansion of SFQED must be resummed to extract sensible physical predictions
[
16
,
17
,
18
]
. Going beyond the background field approximation, and treating depletion analytically is, however, challenging
[
9
,
19
]
.
There have been some attempts to investigate how a pair-creating field depletes in scalar QED – see
[
20
]
, for QED
2
see
[
21
,
22
]
, and see
[
23
]
for QCD.

Here we approach the topic of backreaction slowly but systematically, staying as close as possible to standard methods of SFQED. We take the strong field to be initially described by a coherent state of
photons.
We evolve this state in time, and calculate observables as expectation values in the time-evolved state: we emphasise that we make no assumption on how the coherent state evolves, whereas in the standard approach to SFQED one effectively assumes it remains unchanged, which is equivalent to assuming a fixed background
[
24
,
25
,
26
]
. As such we will be able to observe changes to the coherent state, i.e. backreaction on the initial strong field. Our focus will be on initial coherent states which can spontaneously create particle/anti-particle pairs (via the Schwinger effect), hence state evolution will clearly be non-trivial. A coherent state approach to depletion has been adopted in the past: in
[
27
]
depletion in laser-matter interactions was modelled in an SFQED context as a deformation of the initial coherent state; and in
[
28
,
29
]
backreaction was associated to corrections in the inverse of the expected coherent state occupation number,
N
about the large
N
limit of scattering processes. See
[
30
]
for an exactly solvable toy model of backreaction using coherent states.

In order to perform these calculations we need an expansion scheme, which we choose so as to parallel the Furry expansion (in which, concretely, the background is fixed but treated exactly, while quantised photons are treated with the familiar perturbative expansion in the fine structure constant)
[
6
]
. This approach will have the benefit of allowing us to match to familiar objects, while seeing explicitly what the background field approximation misses.

We will take an amplitudes-based approach to this material, which will expose a great deal of structure, and apply our methods to observables that are not often considered in SFQED.
One such observable, and one which naturally exhibits effects due to backreaction, is the
waveform
, the leading asymptotic form of (the expectation value of) the electromagnetic field
F_{\mu\nu}
in the system. The corresponding gravitational waveform is commonly studied in the context of the classical two-body problem
[
31
,
32
,
33
]
. Waveforms generated by purely quantum effects, on the other hand, are less commonly explored.

In terms of structure, we will see that, due to the possibility of particle creation, observables pick up contributions from
non-trivial
bubble diagrams which mix the loop and Furry expansions. This implies non-trivial disconnected contributions to observables, which are also not often considered in SFQED due to the common use of plane-wave fields, which cannot produce pairs
[
34
]
. Furthermore, this investigation will provide us with an accessible model in which to explore resummation. This is a topic which has arisen in many SFQED contexts in recent years, including the infra-red
[
35
]
, effective actions
[
36
]
, bubble chains
[
37
,
38
]
, collinear contributions
[
39
]
, resurgence
[
40
,
41
]
, radiation reaction
[
42
,
43
,
44
]
, and particle states
[
45
,
46
]
.

This paper is organised as follows. In Sec.
II
we review the calculation of scattering amplitudes involving coherent states, and show how resulting observables may be related to familiar background-field amplitudes, and calculated order by order in analogy to the Furry expansion. In subsequent sections we explore various observables in this expansion. Working first to leading order in Sec
III
, we discuss pair production and relate the amplitudes-based approach to that based on Bolgoliubov transformations. We proceed to next-to leading order (NLO) in Sec
IV
, where backreaction on the driving fields first appears. We consider first the waveform, in which interference effects signal deviations from the background-field approximation. To give a completely explicit example, we calculate the waveform explicitly in a simple pair-producing field model. In Sec.
V
we re-analyse several of our results in the context of resummation, asking how our results are built from Feynman diagrams. We will see that even well-known results such as the vacuum persistence amplitude and the number of created pairs exhibit an extremely rich structure in terms of disconnected and cut diagrams. We conclude in Sec.
VI
. For simplicity, we restrict to scalar QED throughout, and we set
\hbar=c=1
.

II
Setup and review

The physical effects of interest in this paper arise in the presence of strong electromagnetic fields. To model these fields we take the initial scattering state to be a
coherent state
of photons
[
47
,
48
,
24
,
25
]
. Being of minimal uncertainty and arbitrarily high occupancy
[
49
]
, these states are a natural choice to describe classical fields. We begin by briefly reviewing the necessary properties of coherent states.

II.1
The initial coherent state

In terms of the mode operator
a_{s}(k)
and polarisation
\varepsilon^{s}_{\mu}(k)
for photons of momentum
k
and helicity
s
, the mode expansion of the free electromagnetic field is

\displaystyle A_{\mu}(x)=\int_{k}\big[a_{s}(k)\varepsilon^{s}_{\mu}(k)e^{-ik\cdot x}+a^{\dagger}_{s}(k){\bar{\varepsilon}}^{s}_{\mu}(k)e^{ik\cdot x}\big]\;,

(1)

where an integral with subscript denotes the usual on-shell momenta integral,

\int_{k}\equiv\int\!\frac{{\mathrm{d}}^{3}{\mathbf{k}}}{(2\pi)^{3}2k_{0}}\;,

(2)

with
k_{0}
the on-shell energy (for photons or, below, massive fields as appropriate). To avoid the appearance of many sums, we adopt the convention that if the helicity label (
s
,
\sigma
, etc) appears twice in any expression then it is summed over,
whether it appears as an index or otherwise
. The mode operators obey
[a_{s}(k),a^{\dagger}_{\sigma}(k^{\prime})]=\delta_{s\sigma}{\delta}_{k^{\prime}k}
, in which
\delta_{k^{\prime}k}
should be understood as the on-shell delta function, i.e.
~\int_{k}\delta_{k^{\prime}k}=1
. A coherent state of photons may then be written
[
48
,
49
]

\ket{z}\equiv\mathcal{D}(z)\ket{0}\;,\qquad\mathcal{D}(z)=\exp\bigg[\int_{k}a^{\dagger}_{s}(k)z_{s}(k)-a_{s}(k){\bar{z}}_{s}(k)\bigg]\;,

(3)

in which
z_{s}(k)
are two complex profile functions.
Coherent states are not eigenstates of number operators
N(k)=a_{s}^{\dagger}(k)a_{s}(k)
; rather, exponentiation means that they are made up of states states with arbitrary high occupation numbers. Taking the expectation value of (
1
) in the coherent state
|z\rangle
simply replaces the mode operators by the profile functions,
z_{s}(k)
, yielding a classical vacuum solution of Maxwell’s equations associated to this choice of profile:

\bra{z}A_{\mu}(x)\ket{z}=\int_{k}\big[z_{s}(k)\varepsilon^{s}_{\mu}(k)e^{-ik\cdot x}+{\bar{z}}_{s}(k){\bar{\varepsilon}}^{s}_{\mu}(k)e^{ik\cdot x}\big]=:A^{\text{cl}}_{\mu}(x)\;.

(4)

For example, the choice
z_{s}(k)\sim\delta(k_{1})\delta(k_{2})f(k_{3})
will lead to a plane wave
A_{\mu}^{\text{cl}}
with pulse profile determined by
f(k_{3})
, while less singular choices of
z_{s}(k)
lead to spatially focussed fields, see e.g.
[
50
]
.
The essential property of the ‘displacement operator’
\mathcal{D}(z)
required for all calculations with coherent states is that it acts on mode operators as
[
49
]

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger}[z]a_{s}(k)\mathcal{D}[z]

\displaystyle=a_{s}(k)+z_{s}(k)\;.

(5)

This in turn implies that for an arbitrary functional
\mathcal{F}
of the gauge field, we have the relation

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger}[z]\mathcal{F}[A]\mathcal{D}[z]=\mathcal{F}[A+A^{\text{cl}}]\;.

(6)

This shift of the dependence on a quantised field by a classical field configuration can be interpreted as introducing a background field in which QED processes take place.

II.2
The final time-evolved state

We evolve the initial state
|z\rangle
, defined above, in time using the QED
S
-matrix operator
S
. The state evolves non-trivially, due to quantum effects, into the asymptotic final state

\ket{\text{out}}:=S\ket{z}\;,

(7)

which packages all information needed to compute asymptotic observables. (Throughout this paper we assume that we are working with a renormalised Lagrangian and
S
-matrix at least up to a suitable order in the Furry expansion.) To investigate this final state we expose the displacement operator in
\ket{z}
, writing
\ket{\text{out}}=S\mathcal{D}[z]\ket{0}

and then multiply by unity in the form
1=\mathcal{D}[z]\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}[z]
. This allows us to use (
6
) to turn
S
into
S[A^{\text{cl}}]
, the QED
S
-matrix in the background
A^{\text{cl}}
defined in (
4
)
[
24
,
25
,
26
,
27
]
:

\ket{\text{out}}=\mathcal{D}[z]\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}[z]S\mathcal{D}[z]\ket{0}=\mathcal{D}[z]S[A^{\text{cl}}]\ket{0}\;.

(8)

In order to express the final state itself in terms of objects familiar from strong field QED, namely scattering amplitudes in the classical background
A^{\text{cl}}
, we insert a complete set of asymptotic states
\ket{f}
between the remaining
\mathcal{D}[z]
and
S[A^{\text{cl}}]
, which yields

\begin{split}\ket{\text{out}}&=\sum_{f}\mathcal{D}[z]\ket{f}\bra{f}S[A^{\text{cl}}]\ket{0}=:\mathcal{D}[z]\sum_{f}\ket{f}\mathcal{M}(0\to f;A^{\text{cl}})\;.\end{split}

(9)

Here
\mathcal{M}(0\to f;A^{\text{cl}})
is, as desired, the
S
-matrix element for transitions, from vacuum to
\ket{f}
, in the background
A^{\text{cl}}
. In the Furry expansion this matrix element would be calculated by treating the background-field dependence
exactly
(incorporated into the asymptotic states of the theory and the particle propagators), but using the usual perturbative expansion with respect to quantised photons that appear as external scattering states or internal lines, where the (naive
[
16
,
17
]
) coupling remains the fine structure constant.

II.3
Observables

We compute observables as expectation values of operators
\hat{O}
in the final state. Using the representation (
9
) allows us to express these observables in terms of background field amplitudes. There are broadly two cases to consider. First, suppose that the observable of interest acts only on the matter degrees of freedom. Then
\hat{O}
commutes with the remaining displacement operator in (
9
) and we have

\bra{\text{out}}\hat{O}\ket{\text{out}}\to\sum_{f^{\prime},f}\bar{\mathcal{M}}(0\to f^{\prime};A^{\text{cl}})\bra{f^{\prime}}\hat{O}\ket{f}\mathcal{M}(0\to f;A^{\text{cl}})\;.

(10)

An example observable is the number of pairs produced. In this case the asymptotic free states
\ket{f}
are eigenstates of
\ket{O}
, so the double sum collapses to a single sum, and all contributions come from cuts of higher loop amplitudes.

The second case is that
\hat{O}
acts also on photon degrees of freedom, so
\hat{O}\equiv\hat{O}(a^{\dagger},a)
(suppressing spin and momentum labels), then the displacement operator generates additional terms, and we have

\bra{\text{out}}\hat{O}(a^{\dagger},a)\ket{\text{out}}\to\sum_{f^{\prime},f}\bar{\mathcal{M}}(0\to f^{\prime};A^{\text{cl}})\bra{f^{\prime}}\hat{O}(a^{\dagger}+{\bar{z}},a+z)\ket{f}\mathcal{M}(0\to f;A^{\text{cl}})\;.

(11)

An example of such an observable is the waveform, i.e. the profile function of the leading
1/r
falloff of the radiated electromagnetic field in the asymptotic future. Following
[
31
]
, this takes the form

W_{\mu\nu}(u,{\bf n})=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\!\hat{{\mathrm{d}}}\omega\,e^{-i\omega u}\,k_{[\mu}\varepsilon^{\sigma}_{\nu]}\bra{\text{out}}a_{\sigma}(k)\ket{\text{out}}+\text{c.c.}\Big|_{k=\omega(1,{\bf n})}\;,

(12)

where
{\bf n}
defines the point of observation on the 2-sphere at infinity (i.e. it contains two angular degrees of freedom) while
u
is retarded time. It is clearly sufficient to work with the spectral, or frequency domain, waveform

f_{\mu\nu}(\omega,{\bf n}):=k_{[\mu}\varepsilon^{\sigma}_{\nu]}\bra{\text{out}}a_{\sigma}(k)\ket{\text{out}}\Big|_{k=\omega(1,{\bf n})}\;.

(13)

We end with a comment that we have not made any background field approximation – all we have done is taken an initial coherent state and let it evolve in time. Nevertheless, our observables can still be expressed in terms of familiar background field amplitudes, plus additional terms as in e.g. (
11
). In the following sections we consider observables, and therefore the background field amplitudes, order by order in the Furry expansion.

III
Leading order in the Furry expansion

In this and the following sections we construct the final state
\ket{\text{out}}
explicitly, order by order in the Furry expansion, analyse its structure, and use it to investigate observables. We begin at zeroth order – here there is no backreaction on the initial coherent state, but there is nontrivial physics in the form of pair production.
The material in this section is largely straightforward, and to some extent already appears in the literature, in different guises
[
51
,
52
,
53
,
54
,
55
]
, but our approach is different and the calculations will serve to fix notation and illustrate useful lessons.

To zeroth order in the Furry expansion the only
\ket{f}
which can contribute to the sums in (
9
) and (
11
) are (scalar) electron-positron pair states. (Spontaneous photon creation can occur only at higher order, see below.)
The resummed form of the series is well-known – the vacuum evolves to a
squeezed
state
[
49
,
52
]
. This state may be parameterised as, writing
\mathcal{O}_{F}
to denote the order of our Furry-like expansion,

\ket{\text{out}}=\mathcal{D}[z]\,e^{i\mathcal{W}_{\hskip 0.43054pt\hbox to3.12pt{\vbox to3.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.56111pt\lower-1.56111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.19 0 C 1.19 0.66 0.66 1.19 0 1.19 C -0.66 1.19 -1.19 0.66 -1.19 0 C -1.19 -0.66 -0.66 -1.19 0 -1.19 C 0.66 -1.19 1.19 -0.66 1.19 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.19 0 C 1.19 0.66 0.66 1.19 0 1.19 C -0.66 1.19 -1.19 0.66 -1.19 0 C -1.19 -0.66 -0.66 -1.19 0 -1.19 C 0.66 -1.19 1.19 -0.66 1.19 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope

(14)

in which the constant
\mathcal{W}_{\hskip 0.60275pt\hbox to3.81pt{\vbox to3.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.90555pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.67 0 C 1.67 0.92 0.92 1.67 0 1.67 C -0.92 1.67 -1.67 0.92 -1.67 0 C -1.67 -0.92 -0.92 -1.67 0 -1.67 C 0.92 -1.67 1.67 -0.92 1.67 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.67 0 C 1.67 0.92 0.92 1.67 0 1.67 C -0.92 1.67 -1.67 0.92 -1.67 0 C -1.67 -0.92 -0.92 -1.67 0 -1.67 C 0.92 -1.67 1.67 -0.92 1.67 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope

and the kernel
\mathcal{A}_{\hskip 0.60275pt\hbox to4.41pt{\vbox to4.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.20694pt\lower-2.20694pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{{{{}{}{}{}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -2.09 L 2.09 -2.09}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -2.09 L 2.09 -2.09}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
are fully determined by the
S
-matrix (equivalently the Schrödinger equation) and we have introduced the electron and positron creation operators
b^{\dagger}(p)
and
d^{\dagger}(q)
. We would like, though, to express these quantities directly in terms of amplitudes. This is done by taking the overlap with e.g. the vacuum
\ket{0}
and a single pair state
\ket{p,q}

determines the vacuum persistence amplitude (
15
) – it is the one-loop effective action of the field
A^{\text{cl}}
. Without the initial coherent state, the effective action would be the free fermion bubble, contributing an irrelevant phase that could be divided out. Here, though,
\mathcal{W}_{\hskip 0.60275pt\hbox to3.81pt{\vbox to3.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.90555pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.67 0 C 1.67 0.92 0.92 1.67 0 1.67 C -0.92 1.67 -1.67 0.92 -1.67 0 C -1.67 -0.92 -0.92 -1.67 0 -1.67 C 0.92 -1.67 1.67 -0.92 1.67 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.67 0 C 1.67 0.92 0.92 1.67 0 1.67 C -0.92 1.67 -1.67 0.92 -1.67 0 C -1.67 -0.92 -0.92 -1.67 0 -1.67 C 0.92 -1.67 1.67 -0.92 1.67 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
can develop an imaginary part, which via the optical theorem indicates the possibility of pair creation – it is not an irrelevant phase and should not be divided out, at least not in its entirety.

Turning to (
16
), the amplitude for production of a single pair is the
product
of the vacuum persistence amplitude with
\mathcal{A}_{\hskip 0.60275pt\hbox to4.41pt{\vbox to4.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.20694pt\lower-2.20694pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{{{{}{}{}{}}}
{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}

. The former will appear in all amplitudes we calculate; if we write a given amplitude as a product

s becomes familiar scattering amplitudes in the vacuum limit, as computed using the usual Feynman rules. Hence the

{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 11.81 L 11.81 11.81}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 11.81 L 11.81 11.81}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -11.81 L 11.81 -11.81}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -11.81 L 11.81 -11.81}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
{}{{}}{}{{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{{}{}{}{}}}
{{}{}{}{}}}}{}
{}
{}
{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 11.81 C -6.52 11.81 -11.81 6.52 -11.81 0 C -11.81 -6.52 -6.52 -11.81 0 -11.81}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 11.81 C -6.52 11.81 -11.81 6.52 -11.81 0 C -11.81 -6.52 -6.52 -11.81 0 -11.81}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(18)

Amplitudes are therefore
not
represented only by the corresponding connected Feynman diagrams – the vacuum persistance factor is always present. This is known, see e.g.
[
52
]
(though that source deals mainly with ‘reduced’ amplitudes in which the vacuum persistence factor is divided out!) but is not often encountered in SFQED because of the dominance of the plane wave model, where
there is no spontaneous particle production. As we consider more complicated processes or initial states, more types of disconnected contributions appear, as we will see.

At this order in the Furry picture, the only non-trivial amplitudes in our theory are the
1\to 1
amplitudes, the
0\to 2
pair production amplitude discussed above, and the
2\to 0
pair annihilation amplitude. All other (asymptotic) quantities must be expressible in terms of these – the vacuum persistence amplitude, for example, is clearly determined (up to a phase) in terms of the
0\to 2
amplitude by unitarity. We consider this example, and others, below.

III.1
Example: Bogoliubov from amplitudes and crossing symmetry

For our first example we revisit the well-known Bogoliubov description of scalar pair creation, reformulating it in terms of amplitudes. In the Bogoliubov approach one observes that because the
S
-matrix is essentially a squeezing operator, the time-evolved ladder operators
B(p)=S^{\dagger}b(p)S
and
D(p)=S^{\dagger}d(p)S
must be related to
b(p)
and
d(p)
through a linear transformation of the form

\begin{split}B(p)=\int_{q}\alpha_{pq}b(q)+\beta_{pq}d^{\dagger}(q)\;,\qquad D^{\dagger}_{p}=\int_{q}\alpha^{\dagger}_{pq}d^{\dagger}(q)+\beta^{\dagger}_{pq}b(q)\;,\end{split}

(19)

for kernels
\alpha_{pq}
and
\beta_{pq}
– we use a ‘matrix’ notation for pair momenta arguments, such that
\alpha^{\dagger}_{pq}\equiv{\bar{\alpha}}_{qp}
, and so on. The unitarity of the transformation between
b,d
and
B,D
, implying
b=SBS^{\dagger}
etc, gives us the relations

(\alpha^{\dagger}\alpha)_{pq}-(\beta\beta^{\dagger})_{pq}=\delta_{pq}\;,\qquad(\alpha\beta)_{pq}=(\beta\alpha)_{pq}\;.

(20)

This allow us to easily invert (
19
), yielding

\begin{split}b(p)=\int_{q}\alpha^{\dagger}_{pq}B(q)-\beta_{pq}D^{\dagger}(q)\;,\qquad d^{\dagger}_{p}=\int_{q}\alpha_{pq}D^{\dagger}(q)-\beta^{\dagger}_{pq}B(q)\;,\end{split}

(21)

which is used in what follows. One must also define, in this approach, a reference state
\ket{\mathbb{0}}=S^{\dagger}\ket{\text{in}}
. This state is annihilated by
B(p)
and
D(p)
, but appears to have no relevance to amplitudes because it evolves the initial state
backward
in time. However, by rewriting amplitudes in terms of
B
,
D
and
\ket{\mathbb{0}}
, one can explicitly relate them to the Bogoliubov coefficients. To illustrate, we can write the
1\to 1
(electron) and pair production amplitudes as

\begin{split}\bra{\text{in}}b(q)Sb^{\dagger}(p)\ket{\text{in}}&=\bra{\mathbb{0}}B(q)b^{\dagger}(p)\ket{\text{in}}\,\\
\bra{\text{in}}b(p)d(q)S\ket{\text{in}}&=\bra{\mathbb{0}}B(p)D(q)\ket{\text{in}}\;,\end{split}

(22)

which, using (
19
) and (
21
), and writing the
1\to 1
electron/positron diagrams, momentum
p\to q
, as
\mathcal{A}_{\hskip 0.3014pt\hbox to4.02pt{\vbox to2.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-1.19458pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.88 1.38 L 2.92 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.88 -1.38 L 2.92 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(p,q)
/

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0.32 L 5 0.32}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -0.32 L 5 -0.32}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.09 0 L 3.13 1.38}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.09 0 L 3.13 -1.38}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(23)

Note that the vacuum persistence amplitude has dropped out of these expressions, thus
\alpha
and

are expressible in terms of

. This is still somewhat abstract; the physics in the Bogoliubov coefficients is more clearly revealed by thinking of them as operators acting on amplitudes, or more specifically
diagrams
. Consider for example writing the

(26)

There is an asymmetry in these expressions compared to (
24
)–(
25
). The additional
\alpha
-dependent term is required because, physically, there is always a
1\to 1
amplitude
1
1

1

For subtleties with this statement related to the choice of time coordinate, and lightfront zero modes, see
[
56
,
57
]
.
, but there may not be a
0\to 2
amplitude, depending on whether or not the field can pair-create; if it cannot, then
\beta=0
and
\alpha^{\dagger}=\alpha^{-1}
, consistent with (
20
) and (
23
). Note that these relations are
exact, since the linear transformation between the creation and annihilation operators is determined by the

S
-matrix. For a discussion of analogous results in a gravitational context, and an application to Hawking radiation, see
[
58
]
.

IV
Next to leading order

At next-to-leading order (NLO), that is
\mathcal{O}_{F}(e)
in the Furry expansion, photon modes can be populated in the final state, contributing to backreaction on the initial coherent state. We need two more diagrams, specifically

(28)

The ‘vacuum emission’ diagram (
27
) describes the generation of photon modes not present in the classical field defined by the initial coherent state
[
59
]
. In other words, the average electromagnetic field in the evolved state differs from that expected by solving the vacuum Maxwell equations, due to quantum effects. (For recent work on these “tadpole diagrams” in the presence of constant electromagnetic fields see
[
60
,
61
,
62
,
63
,
64
]
.). The radiation diagram (
28
) describes the production of a pair along with a photon – one of the physical effects encoded here is simply the radiation of a created pair when it is accelerated by the field of the coherent state.

Though working only to NLO, it is convenient to write contributions to the time-evolved state up to this order in exponential form as

(29)

which is formally correct to
\mathcal{O}_{F}(e)
. (See e.g.
[
65
,
66
]
for investigations of ‘the exponential S-matrix’.) The structure of the state so written is as follows. First, there is no correction to the vacuum persistence amplitude to this order, so
\braket{0|\text{out}}
is still given by (
15
), which appears explicitly in the first line of (
29
) along with an tadpole contribution which describes the possibility of vacuum emission. This resembles a coherent state operator, which we return to in a moment. In the second line of (
29
), we see a squeezing operator as at LO, describing pair production, except that the associated squeezed state profile receives an
operator-valued
shift: this elegeantly captures the fact that produced pairs can now radiate photons.

To see this even more clearly, we point out that the
\mathcal{O}_{F}(e)
state can be arrived at from the
\mathcal{O}_{F}(e^{0})
state by making the formal shift
\bar{z}_{\sigma}(k)\rightarrow\bar{z}_{\sigma}(k)+a^{\dagger}_{\sigma}(k)
and keeping only
linear
corrections in
a_{\sigma}^{\dagger}
in the exponent, which is equivalent to retaining one power of
e
. This shift induces the changes

in which differentiation of e.g. the vacuum persistence loop with respect to
z
essentially pulls out one photon, turning it into the tadpole. This results in the
operator-valued
shifts in the state.

,

\ell
, helicity
s

z_{s}(\ell)=0
, we have

(32)

The radiation diagram appears in the first term. The second term is a
disconnected
contribution in which the pair is produced without radiation, while the photon comes from the vacuum emission process already discussed above. Note that both terms in (
32
) are of the same order in the Furry expansion, but of different loop orders. Similar statements apply to other processes – the
\mathcal{O}_{F}(e)
diagrams contributing to nonlinear Compton scattering (photon emission from an electron), for example, are the connected three-point diagram, and the disconnected product of the
1\to 1
diagram with the tadpole diagram:

(33)

Returning to the structure of the state (
29
), a simple reorganisation of terms takes the emphasis away from squeezing and onto changes to the initial coherent state, i.e. backreaction on the state.
To this end, we transport the displacement operator
\mathcal{D}[z]
back toward
\ket{0}
, writing the final state as

(35)

The state has the form
\hat{O}\ket{z}
, in which
\hat{O}
is an operator in the asymptotic future, which we can interpret as parameterising the deformation of the initial state due to quantum effects. In particular, the final exponential factor in (
34
) takes the form of a displacement operator, with operator-valued profile
\mathcal{B}
, but
defined with respect to vacuum
\ket{z}
and acting on
\ket{z}
, hence the appearance of the shifted operator
a^{\dagger}-{\bar{z}}
. (Note: the conjugate term
B^{\dagger}(k;\sigma)\big(a_{\sigma}(k)-z_{\sigma}(k)\big)
vanishes acting on
\ket{z}
, while associated normalisation factor can be dropped as it is higher order in
e
.) In this sense, that same exponential creates (shifted) photon excitations on the background
z
, which sounds like what is expected in background field perturbation theory. However, as we will see in the following section, the shifts can be responsible for interference effects which are missed in the background field approximation.

We can also interpret this from the perspective of the vacuum by noting that the product of operators
\hat{O}D[z]
can be decomposed into an operator-valued normalisation factor multiplied by a new displacement operator
D[z^{\prime}]
where
z^{\prime}_{\sigma}=z_{\sigma}(k)+\mathcal{B}(k;\sigma)
, again to first order. In this way the photon sector remains, to this order, a coherent state in the asymptotic future, but becomes augmented by excitations of the matter field. Further below we will analyse the functional form of the final state via the waveform.

We comment that the use of an exponential representation of the final state is clearly convenient at this order in the Furry expansion. Questions of how to extend this approach to higher orders could be informed by investigations of exponentiation, and quantum remainders, in eikonal scattering
[
67
,
68
,
69
,
70
,
71
,
72
,
73
,
74
,
75
]
, for a review see
[
76
]
.

29
), it is straightforward to obtain information about observables to
\mathcal{O}_{F}(e)
. We begin with the spectral waveform (
13
), the field-dependent piece of which is contained in the expectation value of the photon mode operator. Using (
29
) we immediately obtain

(36)

The first term is the
O(e^{0})
result that the waveform is just that of the original coherent state – the initial field evolves according to the classical Maxwell’s equations in vacuum. The second and third terms correct this through, respectively, vacuum emission and the radiation produced by created pairs. The structure of the final term is particularly interesting; the
amplitude
for radiation from a pair with momenta
\{p,q\}
appears integrated against the
expectation value
of the pair creation operator with respect to the final state; this is because any number of pairs can be produced at order
\mathcal{O}_{F}(e^{0})
, which may then go on to radiate. Evaluating the expectation value is simplified, along with calculations and presentation below, by adopting and expanding on the ‘matrix’ notation described after (
19
), applied to all
pair
momenta indices. To illustrate:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.09 L 2.09 2.09}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.09 L 2.09 2.09}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -2.09 L 2.09 -2.09}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -2.09 L 2.09 -2.09}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{A}}}^{\dagger}_{\hskip 0.60275pt\hbox to4.41pt{\vbox to4.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.20694pt\lower-2.20694pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{{{{}{}{}{}}}
{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.09 C -1.15 2.09 -2.09 1.15 -2.09 0 C -2.09 -1.15 -1.15 -2.09 0 -2.09}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.09 C -1.15 2.09 -2.09 1.15 -2.09 0 C -2.09 -1.15 -1.15 -2.09 0 -2.09}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.09 L 2.09 2.09}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.09 L 2.09 2.09}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -2.09 L 2.09 -2.09}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -2.09 L 2.09 -2.09}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}})^{-1}\Big]\\
&=:z_{\sigma}(k)+\delta z_{\sigma}(k)\;.\end{split}

(38)

The structure of the radiation term is the most complicated. It receives contributions from diagrams comprised of an arbitrary numbers of
disconnected
pair production diagrams, but where (at this order) only one of which produces radiation, while the rest contribute a non-trivial factor. This highlights the importance of disconnected contributions in strong field regimes.

A purely background field calculation would not recover the leading term in (
36
), which is not surprising. It has, however, non-trivial consequences at higher orders and for other observables. We can illustrate this using the average number of the produced photons
N_{\gamma}
, easily found to be

\displaystyle\braket{N_{\gamma}}=\int_{k}\braket{\text{out}|\,a^{\dagger}_{\sigma}(k)a_{\sigma}(k)|\text{out}}=\int_{k}{\bar{z}}_{\sigma}(k)z_{\sigma}(k)+\Big[{\bar{z}}_{\sigma}(k)\delta z_{\sigma}(k)+z_{\sigma}(k)\delta{\bar{z}}_{\sigma}(k)\Big]+\mathcal{O}_{F}(e^{2})\;.

(39)

Beyond the leading order term, we find the same
\delta z_{\sigma}(k)
as appears in the waveform, which appears through the interference of
O(e^{0})
and
O(e^{1})
amplitudes. As such, the information contained in
\delta z
is no longer ‘painted on the sky’ as in the waveform, but is integrated against the initial coherent state profile, only contributing when photons are emitted, via vacuum emission or radiation from pairs, into the same part of momentum space as occupied by the initial coherent state. Such terms are missed in the background field approach, as is easily demonstrated by comparison with
\langle N_{\gamma}\rangle
calculated therein, which takes the schematic form

\langle N_{\gamma}\rangle\overset{\text{background}}{\sim}\sum_{n}n|\mathcal{A}(0\to n)|^{2}\;,

(40)

i.e. it comes entirely from background field amplitudes mod-squared. The leading contribution is thus at
\mathcal{O}_{F}(e^{2})
(coming from the mod-squared tadpole and radiation amplitudes
[
52
]
). While it is again unsurprising that the background-field approach would not count the
\mathcal{O}_{F}(e^{0})
number of photons in the initial coherent state, we see clearly the consequence that the non-trivial
\mathcal{O}_{F}(e)
interference terms in (
39
) are also missed.

We can view these interference terms as generalised colinear contributions. Practically, their observation would be difficult in any strong field experiment: for example, most detector equipment placed in the path of a high intensity laser would be severely ablated, making precision measurements challenging. The waveform and other ‘off-axis’ observables are therefore more interesting, from an experimental point of view.

IV.2
Example 2: all-orders waveform in the impulsive limit

As a concrete example of the formalism outlined above, we study here the example

\displaystyle eA_{\mu}=\delta_{\mu}^{0}\delta(x^{0})\,\delta p\cdot x\;,

(41)

in which
\delta p_{\mu}
is a space-like momentum vector. This potential generates a time-dependent electric field with delta function temporal profile and polarisation in the direction of
\delta p_{\mu}
. It can be obtained from a finite-duration pulse, e.g. a Sauter pulse, by taking the short-duration, high-field limit, see
[
77
]
. We will for the field (
41
) be able to explicitly calculate the pair production and photon emission diagrams and amplitudes above.

As usual, all information on the system’s dynamics at leading order in the Furry expansion is contained in the positive/negative energy scalar mode functions of the Klein-Gordon equation in the background.
Thanks to the simple form of the background, the system is integrable at the leading order, we can construct explicitly two sets of mode functions corresponding to free modes in either the asymptotic past (
in
-modes) or asymptotic future (
out
-modes). The
in
-modes are given by

\displaystyle\phi^{+}_{(in)\mathbf{p}}(x)

\displaystyle=\Theta(-x^{0}){e^{-ip\cdot x}}+e^{-iP_{i}x^{i}}\Theta(x^{0})\sqrt{\frac{p_{0}}{P_{0}}}\Big(\tilde{\alpha}_{\mathbf{p}}{e^{-iP_{0}x^{0}}}-\tilde{\beta}_{\mathbf{p}}{e^{+iP_{0}x^{0}}}\Big)\;,

(42)

\displaystyle\phi^{-}_{(in)-\mathbf{p}}(x)

\displaystyle=\Theta(-x^{0}){e^{ip_{0}x^{0}-ip_{i}x^{i}}}+e^{-iP_{i}x^{i}}\Theta(x^{0})\sqrt{\frac{p_{0}}{P_{0}}}\Big(\tilde{\alpha}_{\mathbf{p}}{e^{iP_{0}x^{0}}}-\tilde{\beta}_{\mathbf{p}}{e^{-iP_{0}x^{0}}}\Big)\;,

(43)

where
p_{0}=\sqrt{\mathbf{p}^{2}+m^{2}}
,
(P_{0},P_{i})=(\sqrt{(\mathbf{p}+\delta\mathbf{p})^{2}+m^{2}},p_{i}+\delta p_{i})
and

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}_{\mathbf{p}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{p_{0}}{P_{0}}}+\sqrt{\frac{P_{0}}{p_{0}}}\right)\;,\qquad\tilde{\beta}_{\mathbf{p}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{p_{0}}{P_{0}}}-\sqrt{\frac{P_{0}}{p_{0}}}\right)

(44)

The
out
-modes are given by

\displaystyle\phi^{+}_{(out)\mathbf{P}}(x)

\displaystyle=\Theta(x^{0}){e^{-iP\cdot x}}+e^{-ip_{i}x^{i}}\Theta(-x^{0})\sqrt{\frac{P_{0}}{p_{0}}}\Big(\tilde{\alpha}_{\mathbf{p}}{e^{-ip_{0}x^{0}}}+\tilde{\beta}_{\mathbf{p}}{e^{ip_{0}x^{0}}}\Big)\;,

(45)

\displaystyle\phi^{-}_{(out)-\mathbf{P}}(x)

\displaystyle=\Theta(x^{0}){e^{iP_{0}x^{0}-iP_{i}x^{i}}}+e^{-ip_{i}x^{i}}\Theta(-x^{0})\sqrt{\frac{P_{0}}{p_{0}}}\Big(\tilde{\alpha}_{\mathbf{p}}{e^{ip_{0}x^{0}}}+\tilde{\beta}_{\mathbf{p}}{e^{-ip_{0}x^{0}}}\Big)\;,

(46)

where, here and below,
\mathbf{P}:=\mathbf{p}+\delta\mathbf{p}
. As usual, the scalar field can be expanded using either set of modes,

\displaystyle\Phi(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{p}\,\left[b(p)\phi^{+}_{(in)\mathbf{p}}(x)+d^{\dagger}(p)\phi^{-}_{(in)\mathbf{p}}(x)\right]

(47)

\displaystyle=\int_{p}\,\left[B(p)\phi^{+}_{(out)\mathbf{p}}(x)+D^{\dagger}(p)\phi^{-}_{(out)\mathbf{p}}(x)\right]\;,

(48)

where the operators
B,D
are those introduced in Sec.
III.1
. To see this, we use the Heisenberg evolution of the field operator to write

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\Phi(t,\mathbf{x})=\lim_{t\to-\infty}S^{\dagger}\Phi(t,\mathbf{x})S\;,

(49)

and then compare with (
47
) and (
48
), where the definition of the in and out modes yields the asymptotic behaviour

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to-\infty}\Phi(t,\mathbf{x})\sim\int_{p}\,b(p)e^{-ip\cdot x}+d^{\dagger}(p)e^{ip\cdot x}\;,\qquad\lim_{t\to\infty}\Phi(t,\mathbf{x})\sim\int_{p}\,B(p)e^{-ip\cdot x}+D^{\dagger}(p)e^{ip\cdot x}\,.

(50)

It follows directly that
B(p)=S^{\dagger}b(p)S
and
D^{\dagger}(p)=S^{\dagger}d^{\dagger}(p)S
, as previously.

The final ingredient needed to compute amplitudes is the scalar propagator. The
in
-
out
Feynman propagator
G_{+-}(x^{\prime},x)
(i.e. that which naturally arises in an amplitudes context) is

\displaystyle G_{+-}(x^{\prime},x)=\frac{\braket{\mathbb{0}|T\bar{\Phi}(x^{\prime})\Phi(x)|\textrm{in}}}{\braket{\mathbb{0}|\textrm{in}}}

(51)

Expanding the fields in terms of modes as above, we have

\displaystyle G_{+-}(x^{\prime},x)=\int_{p}\sqrt{\frac{p_{0}}{P_{0}}}\frac{1}{\tilde{\alpha}_{\mathbf{p}}}\left[\Theta(x^{\prime 0}-x^{0})\bar{\phi}^{-}_{(out)-\mathbf{P}}(x^{\prime})\phi^{-}_{(in)-\mathbf{p}}(x)+\Theta(x^{0}-x^{\prime 0})\bar{\phi}^{+}_{(in)\mathbf{p}}(x^{\prime})\phi^{+}_{(out)\mathbf{P}}(x)\right]

(52)

We will find in our calculations below that the
in
-
in
propagator
G_{--}(x^{\prime},x)
also arises. It is similarly defined by

\displaystyle G_{--}(x^{\prime},x)

\displaystyle=\braket{\textrm{in}|T\bar{\Phi}(x^{\prime})\Phi(x)|\textrm{in}}

(53)

\displaystyle=\int_{p}\left[\Theta(x^{\prime 0}-x^{0})\bar{\phi}^{-}_{(in)-\mathbf{p}}(x^{\prime})\phi^{-}_{(in)-\mathbf{p}}(x)+\Theta(x^{0}-x^{\prime 0})\bar{\phi}^{+}_{(in)\mathbf{p}}(x^{\prime})\phi^{+}_{(in)\mathbf{p}}(x)\right]

(54)

IV.2.1
Diagrams for
1\to 1
and
0\to 2
processes

The Bogoluibov coefficents
\alpha_{pq}
and
\beta_{pq}
can be computed explicitly for our example. To do so, we rewrite (
43
) and (
42
) as, adopting the compact notation
\hat{{\mathrm{d}}}p={\mathrm{d}}p/2\pi
and
\hat{\delta}(p)=2\pi\delta(p)
,

\displaystyle\phi^{+}_{(in)\bm{\mathbf{p}}}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{q}\tilde{\alpha}_{\mathbf{p}}(2q_{0}){\hat{\delta}^{3}}(\mathbf{p}+\delta\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q})\phi^{+}_{(out)\bm{\mathbf{q}}}(x)-\int_{q}\tilde{\beta}_{\mathbf{p}}(2q_{0}){\hat{\delta}^{3}}(\mathbf{p}+\delta\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{q})\phi^{-}_{(out)\bm{\mathbf{q}}}(x)

(55)

\displaystyle\phi^{-}_{(in)\bm{\mathbf{p}}}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{q}\tilde{\alpha}_{\mathbf{p}}(2q_{0}){\hat{\delta}^{3}}(\mathbf{p}-\delta\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q})\phi^{-}_{(out)\bm{\mathbf{q}}}(x)-\int_{q}\tilde{\beta}_{\mathbf{p}}(2q_{0}){\hat{\delta}^{3}}(\mathbf{p}+\delta\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{q})\phi^{+}_{(out)\bm{\mathbf{q}}}(x)\;.

(56)

Substituting into (
47
) and comparing with (
48
), we find

\displaystyle\alpha_{pq}=\tilde{\alpha}_{\mathbf{p}}(2q_{0}){\hat{\delta}^{3}}(\mathbf{p}+\delta\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q});\qquad\beta_{pq}=-\tilde{\beta}_{\mathbf{p}}(2q_{0}){\hat{\delta}^{3}}(\mathbf{p}+\delta\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{q}).

(57)

It follows, using the general expressions (
23
) that the diagrams for
1\rightarrow 1
electron/positron scattering

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -0.32 L 5 -0.32}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.09 0 L 3.13 1.38}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(58)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\hskip 0.60275pt\hbox to4.41pt{\vbox to4.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.20694pt\lower-2.20694pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{{{{}{}{}{}}}
{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\tilde{\beta}_{\mathbf{p}}}{\tilde{\alpha}_{\mathbf{p}}}(2p_{0}){\hat{\delta}^{3}}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{q})\;.

(59)

From these explicit expressions it is straightforward to verify the crossing symmetry relations (
24
)–(
26
).

(pair plus radiation). These enter the waveform at future null infinity, (
13
), through (
36
). However, the electromagnetic fields of our chosen background (
41
) are homogeneous in space, which means the current density of created pairs does not have compact support, which violates the usual assumption of asymptotic falloff used in the derivation of (
12
) and (
13
). In our case, we will see instead that the contributing amplitudes are supported only at zero frequency, such that the waveform (
13
) at future
null
infinity actually vanishes. Rather, in our example, information about backreaction is encoded in the electromagnetic field at
time-like
future infinity, so this is the object we will study.

To this end, we note that the retarded field strength
F_{\mu\nu}

sourced
by a current
j_{\mu}
has the form

\displaystyle F_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\int\!\hat{{\mathrm{d}}}^{4}k\left(\frac{-2i}{(k_{0}+i0^{+})^{2}-\bm{\mathbf{k}}^{2}}\right)k_{\left[\mu\right.}j_{\left.\nu\right]}(k)e^{-ik\cdot x}\,.

(60)

F_{\mu\nu}
with the expectation value
\braket{F_{\mu\nu}}=\braket{\textrm{out}|F_{\mu\nu}|\textrm{out}}
, the relevant current for studying leading-order backreaction is
\delta z_{\mu}(k)
, introduced in (
38
), but where
k_{\mu}
is taken off-shell. The contributing diagrams

should then be understood as off-shell currents. Let us compute these for our chosen background.

The tadpole diagram can be computed from the matrix element of the gauge invariant current density operator as

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\hskip 0.60275pt\hbox to5.42pt{\vbox to2.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.45346pt\lower-1.45346pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#0000FF} {}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.04 0 C 1.39 0 1.56 1.38 1.91 1.38 C 2.16 1.38 2.37 0.71 2.6 0 C 2.82 -0.71 3.04 -1.38 3.29 -1.38 L 5.21 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.04 0 C 1.04 0.58 0.58 1.04 0 1.04 C -0.58 1.04 -1.04 0.58 -1.04 0 C -1.04 -0.58 -0.58 -1.04 0 -1.04 C 0.58 -1.04 1.04 -0.58 1.04 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.04 0 C 1.04 0.58 0.58 1.04 0 1.04 C -0.58 1.04 -1.04 0.58 -1.04 0 C -1.04 -0.58 -0.58 -1.04 0 -1.04 C 0.58 -1.04 1.04 -0.58 1.04 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope

\displaystyle=\frac{ie\int{\mathrm{d}}^{4}x\,e^{ik\cdot x}\braket{\mathbb{0}|T\bar{\Phi}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leftrightarrow}}{{D}}_{\mu}\Phi(x)|\textrm{in}}}{\braket{\mathbb{0}|\textrm{in}}}

(61)

\displaystyle=ie\int\!{\mathrm{d}}^{4}x\,e^{ik\cdot x}\left.\left(D^{(x)}_{\mu}-{\bar{D}}^{(x^{\prime})}_{\mu}\right)G_{+-}(x^{\prime},x)\right\rvert_{x^{\prime}\rightarrow x}\,,

(62)

where, throughout this paper, the coincidence limit of operator products is taken using a symmetric point-splitting prescription. Since the particle wavefunctions and propagator involve only elementary functions, it is straightforward to perform the Fourier transform in (

). One finds the non-zero components

{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.09 C -1.15 2.09 -2.09 1.15 -2.09 0 C -2.09 -1.15 -1.15 -2.09 0 -2.09}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.09 C -1.15 2.09 -2.09 1.15 -2.09 0 C -2.09 -1.15 -1.15 -2.09 0 -2.09}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.09 L 2.09 2.09}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.09 L 2.09 2.09}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -2.09 L 2.09 -2.09}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -2.09 L 2.09 -2.09}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\right]_{\mu}=-ie\int\!\hat{{\mathrm{d}}}\mathbf{p}\frac{\tilde{\beta}_{\mathbf{p}}}{\tilde{\alpha}_{\mathbf{p}}}\int d^{4}x\,e^{ik\cdot x}\bar{\phi}^{+}_{(in)\mathbf{p}}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leftrightarrow}}{{D}}_{\mu}\phi^{-}_{(in)-\mathbf{p}}(x)\,.

(66)

Combining the two pieces (
64
) and (
66
), we can determine the off-shell analog of
\delta z_{\sigma}
in (
38
) as

\delta z_{\mu}=ie\int\!{\mathrm{d}}^{4}x\,e^{ik\cdot x}\left.\left(D^{(x)}_{\mu}-{\bar{D}}^{(x^{\prime})}_{\mu}\right)G_{--}(x^{\prime},x)\right\rvert_{x^{\prime}\rightarrow x}\eqqcolon\hat{\delta^{3}}(\mathbf{k})j_{\mu}(k_{0})\,,

(67)

which, we note, is precisely the current one finds in the ‘in-in’ formalism, i.e. the mean current generated by pair production. It is interesting that to obtain this from ‘in-out’ quantities, i.e. amplitudes, one has to combine (
64
) and (
66
) which, formally, contains an infinite sum over in-out diagrams, through the inverse operator appearing in the trace. This is a point we return to in equation (
98
) of Sec.
V.2
.

We find, for the non-vanishing parts of the current

\displaystyle j_{i}(k_{0})=-ie\int_{p}\tilde{\alpha}_{\bm{\mathbf{p}}}\tilde{\beta}_{\bm{\mathbf{p}}}(2p_{i})\left(\frac{1}{k_{0}+2p_{0}+i0^{+}}+\frac{1}{k_{0}-2p_{0}+i0^{+}}\right)\;,

(68)

For a current of the form (
68
),
F_{\mu\nu}
reduces to one of a temporally dependent electric field.
Inserting (
68
) into (
60
), and performing the
k_{0}
integral we pick-up contributions from the poles at
k_{0}=0
and, via
j_{\mu}
, poles at
k_{0}=\pm 2p_{0}
. The non-zero components of the resulting electromagnetic field are

\displaystyle F_{0i}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Im}\int\frac{{\mathrm{d}}^{3}\bm{\mathbf{p}}}{(2\pi)^{3}(\mathbf{p}^{2}+m^{2})}(e\tilde{\alpha}_{\bm{\mathbf{p}}}\tilde{\beta}_{\bm{\mathbf{p}}})p_{i}e^{2i\sqrt{\mathbf{p}^{2}+m^{2}}x^{0}}\;.

(69)

The angular integral can be performed exactly, yielding

F_{0i}=\textrm{Im}\,\frac{\delta p_{i}}{|\delta p|}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!{\mathrm{d}}p\,f(p)\,e^{2ix^{0}\sqrt{p^{2}+m^{2}}}\;,

(70)

in which
f(p)
has a lengthy and unilluminating form. While the remaining integral over
p\equiv|{\bf p}|
is involved, we fortunately only need to evaluate it in a saddle point approximation in order to read off the leading asymptotic behavior of
F_{\mu\nu}
as
x^{0}\rightarrow\infty
. For this we only need the
p\rightarrow 0
behavior of
f(p)
, which is explicitly given by

\displaystyle f(p)=-\frac{e|\delta{\bf p}|m\left(\delta p^{2}+2m^{2}\right)}{{24}\pi^{2}\left(\delta p^{2}+m^{2}\right)^{3/2}}\bigg(\frac{p}{m}\bigg)^{4}+\mathcal{O}(p^{5}/m^{5})\,.

(71)

Finally, the leading asymptotic behaviour of the
back-reacted
electromagnetic field
can be computed via the saddle-point approximation,
and is given by the fairly simple expression

\displaystyle F_{\mu\nu}=({\mathrm{d}}x^{0}\wedge\delta p)_{\mu\nu}\left[\frac{em^{2}\left(\delta p^{2}+2m^{2}\right)}{{4}(2\pi)^{3/2}\left(\delta p^{2}+m^{2}\right)^{3/2}}\right]\frac{\sin\left(2mx^{0}+\frac{\pi}{4}\right)}{(2mx^{0})^{5/2}}+\mathcal{O}((mx^{0})^{-7/2})\,.

(72)

We see that the asymptotic back-reacted field is a damped oscillator satisfying
F^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})+\gamma(x^{0})F^{\prime}(x_{0})+\omega^{2}(x^{0})F(x_{0})=0
, where the time-dependent frequency and damping are respectively
\omega^{2}(x^{0})=\tfrac{15}{4(x^{0})^{2}}+4m^{2}
and
\gamma(u)=\tfrac{5}{x^{0}}
.

V
Resummation

In this section we explore several observables as, at each order in the Furry expansion, resummations of pair creation diagrams. This will expose a great deal of structure.

V.1
Vacuum persistence

We begin by revisiting the vacuum persistence probability
\mathbb{P}
, i.e. the probability of creating no pairs, at leading order in the Furry expansion. This is, from (
29
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}=\left|\braket{\textrm{in}|\textrm{out}}\right|^{2}=\left|e^{i\mathcal{W}_{\hskip 0.43054pt\hbox to3.12pt{\vbox to3.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.56111pt\lower-1.56111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.19 0 C 1.19 0.66 0.66 1.19 0 1.19 C -0.66 1.19 -1.19 0.66 -1.19 0 C -1.19 -0.66 -0.66 -1.19 0 -1.19 C 0.66 -1.19 1.19 -0.66 1.19 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.19 0 C 1.19 0.66 0.66 1.19 0 1.19 C -0.66 1.19 -1.19 0.66 -1.19 0 C -1.19 -0.66 -0.66 -1.19 0 -1.19 C 0.66 -1.19 1.19 -0.66 1.19 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\right|^{2}=e^{-2\textrm{Im}\,\mathcal{W}_{\hskip 0.43054pt\hbox to3.12pt{\vbox to3.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.56111pt\lower-1.56111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.19 0 C 1.19 0.66 0.66 1.19 0 1.19 C -0.66 1.19 -1.19 0.66 -1.19 0 C -1.19 -0.66 -0.66 -1.19 0 -1.19 C 0.66 -1.19 1.19 -0.66 1.19 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.19 0 C 1.19 0.66 0.66 1.19 0 1.19 C -0.66 1.19 -1.19 0.66 -1.19 0 C -1.19 -0.66 -0.66 -1.19 0 -1.19 C 0.66 -1.19 1.19 -0.66 1.19 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\,.

(73)

Unitarity, in the form
\braket{\text{out}|\text{out}}=1
, implies that the same probability can be expressed via

\displaystyle e^{2\text{Im}\,\mathcal{W}_{\hskip 0.43054pt\hbox to3.12pt{\vbox to3.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.56111pt\lower-1.56111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.19 0 C 1.19 0.66 0.66 1.19 0 1.19 C -0.66 1.19 -1.19 0.66 -1.19 0 C -1.19 -0.66 -0.66 -1.19 0 -1.19 C 0.66 -1.19 1.19 -0.66 1.19 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.19 0 C 1.19 0.66 0.66 1.19 0 1.19 C -0.66 1.19 -1.19 0.66 -1.19 0 C -1.19 -0.66 -0.66 -1.19 0 -1.19 C 0.66 -1.19 1.19 -0.66 1.19 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}=\left|\exp\bigg[\int_{p,q}b^{\dagger}(p)d^{\dagger}(q)\mathcal{A}_{\hskip 0.60275pt\hbox to4.41pt{\vbox to4.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.20694pt\lower-2.20694pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{{{{}{}{}{}}}

(76)

We have included the diagrammatic representation of each term – the dashed vertical lines are cuts, across which particle lines are sewn by on-shell momentum integrals (recall (
37
)). There is interesting structure already at order
t^{2}
in this expansion: using the diagrammatic representation, it is clear that the two terms present saturate the distinct ways in which one can sew the product of
two
pair-production (horseshoe) diagrams to their conjugates.

Proceeding to higher orders in the expansion of (
75
) we will obtain a representation of
\mathbb{P}^{-1}
as a sum of all cut diagrams contributing to the probability of creation of all numbers of pairs. The coefficient of
t^{n}
in this series will be the un-normalised probability for the creation of
n
pairs. These higher orders terms quickly become rather complicated, though, so to conveniently organise diagrams contributing to the coefficient of
t^{n}/n!
, we define the cut diagram
\mathcal{B}(p,p^{\prime})
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(p,p^{\prime}):=\int_{q}\mathcal{A}_{\hskip 0.60275pt\hbox to4.41pt{\vbox to4.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.20694pt\lower-2.20694pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{{{{}{}{}{}}}
{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.09 C -1.15 2.09 -2.09 1.15 -2.09 0 C -2.09 -1.15 -1.15 -2.09 0 -2.09}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.09 C -1.15 2.09 -2.09 1.15 -2.09 0 C -2.09 -1.15 -1.15 -2.09 0 -2.09}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.1948pt}{11.43968pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 4.42 15.83)} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,,

(77)

and we will again adopt a matrix representation, writing
\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}_{pp^{\prime}}=\mathcal{B}(p,p^{\prime})
. With this notation, we can rewrite and extend the expansion (
76
) of
\mathbb{b}(t)
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{b}(t)=1+t\,\textrm{Tr}\,\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}+\frac{t^{2}}{2!}\Big((\textrm{Tr}\,\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}})^{2}+\textrm{Tr}\,(\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}^{2})\Big)+...+\mathbb{b}_{n}\frac{t^{n}}{n!}+...

(78)

It is clear that
\mathbb{b}_{n}
, the coefficient of
t^{n}/n!
, will be a polynomial in the set of variables
\{\textrm{Tr}(\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}^{j});1\leq j\leq n\}
, such that the terms take the form

\displaystyle(\textrm{Tr}\,(\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}^{j_{1}}))^{n_{1}}(\textrm{Tr}\,(\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}^{j_{2}}))^{n_{2}}\,\ldots\,(\textrm{Tr}\,(\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}^{j_{r}}))^{n_{r}}\quad;\quad\sum_{i}j_{i}n_{i}=n\,,

(79)

where the constraint simply enforces that the number of pairs contributing to
\mathbb{b}_{n}
is equal to
n
. To completely determine the polynomials
\mathbb{b}_{n}
, however, we also need the exact coefficient of these general terms.

Notice that a typical term of form (
79
) translates to a
disconnected
diagram, made of products of the connected pieces
\textrm{Tr}\,\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}^{j_{i}}
. The coefficient of this term, in the polynomial
\mathbb{b}_{n}
, can be identified with the combinatorial factor counting the number of permutations of the particle/anti-particle legs that give rise to the (topologically) same
disconnected
diagram, which we must identify.

The coefficient of the term shown in (
79
) should itself be a product of two factors, (1) the product of the number of ways
n
can be partitioned into
n_{i}
blocks of size
j_{i}
and (2) the product of the number of permutations of
\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}
possible in each factor of
\textrm{Tr}(\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}^{j_{i}})
. The former factor is well known to be expressible in terms of the coefficients in the so-called Bell polynomial
2
2

2

The Bell polynomial can be generated via
B_{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\left.\left({\frac{\partial}{\partial t}}\right)^{n}\exp\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}{\frac{t^{j}}{j!}}\right)\right|_{t=0}
see e.g.
[
78
]
.
. The latter factor is easily found to be
\prod_{i}(j_{i}-1)!
. (This is not
\prod_{i}j_{i}!
due to the trace, which is invariant under cyclic permutation.) Combining these two factors, we can write the coefficient of
t^{n}/n!
in the form

\displaystyle\mathbb{b}_{n}=B_{n}(\textrm{Tr}[\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}],\textrm{Tr}[\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}^{2}],2!\textrm{Tr}[\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}^{3}],...,(n-1)!\textrm{Tr}[\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}^{n}])

(80)

where,
B_{n}(x_{1},...,x_{n})
is the Bell polynomial
[
78
]
,
and where the factors of
(j_{i}-1)!
in the arguments of the polynomial account for the permutations discussed above.

It is interesting to compare to
[
53
]
, which considered scalar particle production stemming from a current source. The expressions therein for the sum over
n-
particle cut diagrams
agree with the expressions above, upon expanding the Bell polynomials as
B_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\sum_{j_{1}+\dots+nj_{n}=n}\prod^{n}_{i=1}x_{i}^{j_{i}}/(i!)^{j_{i}}j_{i}!
, and then making the replacement
\textrm{Tr}[\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}^{j}]\to jb_{j}/g^{2}
(we refer readers to
[
53
]
for conventions).
That we have the same Bell polynomial structure in LO vacuum persistence, despite considering different models, falls out naturally simply because of the “combinatorics of grouping [
n
] objects into [
j_{i}
] clusters”
[
54
]
. However, we emphasise that in our approach we are not restricted to LO vacuum persistence – higher internal loops and backreaction are incorporated into our setup; indeed, we already highlighted this in the context of interference effects in the waveform, and below we will give the NLO corrections to vacuum persistence.
The series expansion for
\mathbb{b}(t)
can then be written as

\displaystyle\mathbb{b}(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{n}}{n!}B_{n}\left(\textrm{Tr}[\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}],\textrm{Tr}[\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}^{2}],2!\textrm{Tr}[\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}^{3}],...,(n-1)!\textrm{Tr}[\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}^{n}]\right)=\exp\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}t^{j}\frac{\textrm{Tr}[\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}^{j}]}{j}\right)\,,

(81)

where the last line follows from the definition of
B_{n}(x_{1},...,x_{n})

[
78
]
. The series in the exponent can be further simplified to arrive at

\displaystyle\mathbb{b}(t)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\textrm{Tr}\log(1-t\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}})\right)={\det}(1-t\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}})^{-1}\;.

(82)

This reproduces, of course, the expected expression for
\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{b}^{-1}(1)=\det(1-\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}})\;.
What is surprising is the depth of structure in this concise expression, when resolved into Feynman diagrams. It also provides a realisation of unitarity by expressing the imaginary part of the effective action in terms of (forward) scattering into pairs (i.e. optical theorem). Given this, and before moving on to higher orders in the Furry expansion of
\mathbb{P}
, we highlight an interesting corollary of the above.

V.1.1
Pair distributions

The probability of creating exactly
n
pairs can be written explicitly as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{n}=\frac{\det(1-\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}})}{n!}B_{n}(\textrm{Tr}[\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}],\textrm{Tr}[\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}^{2}],2!\textrm{Tr}[\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}^{3}],...,(n-1)!\textrm{Tr}[\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}^{n}])\,,

(83)

which we now explore through some special cases. First, as a check, consider replacing
\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}
with a
\mathbb{C}
-number
\mathcal{B}
. Using
B_{n}(0!,1!,2!,...(n-1)!)=n!
we then correctly recover the statistics of a two-mode squeezed state:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{n}\bigg|_{\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}}=(1-|\mathcal{B}|)|\mathcal{B}|^{n}\;.

(84)

Another special case corresponds to a homogeneous, but time-dependent, field, for which

\displaystyle[\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}^{j}]_{pp^{\prime}}\rightarrow|f(p)|^{2j}(2p_{0})(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{3}(\bm{\mathbf{p}}^{\prime}-\bm{\mathbf{p}})\quad;\quad j=1,2,3...\,,

(85)

where
f(p)
carries the nontrivial information on the pair spectrum. This implies

\displaystyle\textrm{Tr}(\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}^{j})\to\lambda_{j}:=V_{3}\int_{p}(2p_{0})|f(p)|^{2j}\,,

(86)

where
V_{3}=(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{3}(0)
is the spatial volume. In this case, the probability of creating exactly
n
pairs becomes

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n!}\,\exp\left[V_{3}\int_{p}(2p_{0})\log\left(1-|f(p)|^{2}\right)\right]\,B_{n}\left(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},2!\lambda_{3},\ldots\,,(n-1)!\lambda_{n}\right)\,.

(87)

If the probability of creating a pair is small (as in e.g. the weak field regime), and we can impose
\lambda_{j}\ll\lambda_{1}\ll 1\,\forall\,j>1
, then (
87
) can be approximated by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{n}\approx e^{-\lambda_{1}}\frac{\lambda_{1}^{n}}{n!}\;,

(88)

which is the expected Poisson distribution
[
53
,
79
]
. To go beyond this approximation and examine corrections to the Poisson distribution we include effects linear in
\lambda_{2}
. The resulting probability distribution is

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{n}\approx\left(\frac{e^{-\lambda_{1}}}{1+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{2}}\right)\frac{\left(\lambda_{1}^{n}+\binom{n}{2}\,\lambda_{1}^{n-2}\lambda_{2}\right)}{n!}\;,

(89)

which can be interpreted as a one-parameter deformation of the Poisson distribution. (We have left the denominator un-expanded so that (
89
) is explicitly normalised.)
A non-Poissionian distribution was also found in
[
53
]
by studying a variance of the multiplicity distribution and analysing contributing diagrams; a Poissonian distribution could only have resulted if (in our language)
\textrm{Tr}[\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}^{j}]
for
j>1
were neglected.
We shall see in Sec.
V.3
that, at NLO, it is
necessary
to consider the deformed distribution (
89
) to model all the physical effects that arise at
\mathcal{O}_{F}(e^{2})
. This result has potential practical applications in the benchmarking of numerical strong field QED schemes
[
80
,
81
,
82
,
83
]
; as available laser intensity increases, effects related to vacuum polarisation are being added to both PIC and single-particle schemes
[
84
]
, and it would be interesting to test the output of the codes against the distributions above.

The resummation methods above can also be used to compute the leading order particle spectrum; this leads to

dN_{+}(p)=\left(\frac{1}{\mathbb{b}(1)}\frac{\delta\mathbb{b}(1)}{\delta\mathcal{B}(p,p^{\prime})}\Big|_{p^{\prime}\rightarrow p}\right)\frac{{\mathrm{d}}^{3}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}2p_{0}}=\left[\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}\left(1-\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}\right)^{-1}\right]_{pp}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}^{3}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}2p_{0}}\;,

(90)

from which one immediately recovers the expected expression for the average number of pairs,

\displaystyle N=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n\mathbb{P}_{n}=\frac{\dot{\mathbb{b}}(1)}{\mathbb{b}(1)}=\textrm{Tr}\left[\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}\left(1-\bm{\mathbf{\mathcal{B}}}\right)^{-1}\right]\;.

(91)

V.2
Photon number at next-to-leading-order

We have now seen how the function
\mathbb{b}(t)
, defined in (
75
), contains information on the resummation of an infinite number of disconnected diagrams arising from the creation of pairs. Using the experience gained, we will here use
\mathbb{b}(t)
as a generating function to explore related resummation structures in other observables, in particular the number of photons created,
\braket{N_{\gamma}}
. We begin by revisiting the leading order contribution to
\braket{N_{\gamma}}
, asking how the relatively simple expression (
39
) is recovered from the resummation of all contributing diagrams. The contributions to
\braket{N_{\gamma}}
containing
\mathcal{A}_{\hskip 0.60275pt\hbox to5.42pt{\vbox to2.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.45346pt\lower-1.45346pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

(100)

Observe that to arrive at the second line of the above we used (
75
) to cancel the normalisation factor against the infinite series of terms with non-radiating horse-shoes. This emphasises the subtle diagrammatic cancellations which occur in order to, essentially, convert from in-out to in-in quantities.

Notice how we were able, here at LO, to use
\mathbb{b}(1)
as a generating function from which to derive the contribution from all disconnected diagrams. The same approach leads, at next-to-leading order, to the formal expression

\displaystyle\braket{N_{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=\int_{k}{\bar{z}}_{\sigma}(k)z_{\sigma}(k)+\Big[{\bar{z}}_{\sigma}(k)\delta z_{\sigma}(k)+z_{\sigma}(k)\delta{\bar{z}}_{\sigma}(k)\Big]
